# Spectral theory of frame flows on closed hyperbolic manifolds

**arXiv ID**: 2607.29319v1
**Authors**: Julien Namazi
**Published**: 2026-07-31
**Categories**: math.DS, math.AP, math.DG
**Comments**: 56 pages, 1 figure
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.29319v1

## Abstract

We prove a resolvent estimate for the generator of the frame flow on hyperbolic manifolds away from vertical lines of resonances. A byproduct of the proof is an optimal essential spectral gap property for the generator, hence giving another proof of exponential mixing of frame flows with respect to the volume measure of the frame bundle. This extends the result of [https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.08387v2] in dimension 3 to any dimension. We make an extensive use of the Borel-Weil calculus developed in [https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14846] to overcome difficulties of this higher-dimensional case.

## Spectral theory of frame flows on closed hyperbolic manifoldsJulien NamaziUniversité de Paris-Est Créteil , LAMA, 94010, Créteil, France.julien.namazi@cnrs.fr

## Abstract.

We prove a resolvent estimate for the generator of the frame flow on hyperbolic manifolds away from vertical lines of resonances. A byproduct of the proof is an optimal essential spectral gap property for the generator, hence giving another proof of exponential mixing of frame flows with respect to the volume measure of the frame bundle. This extends the result of[DGH21]in dimension33to any dimension. We make an extensive use of the Borel-Weil calculus developed in[CL24]to overcome difficulties of this higher-dimensional case.

## 1.Introduction and main result

LetMMbe a closed manifold. A flow(φt)(\varphi_{t})generated by a vector fieldXXis said to beAnosovif there exists a(d​φt)(d\varphi_{t})invariant splitting(1.1)T​M=ES​Mu⊕ES​Ms⊕ℝ​X,\displaystyle TM=E^{u}_{SM}\oplus E^{s}_{SM}\oplus\mathbb{R}X,

such that there existsC>0C>0andν>0\nu>0satisfying (with respect to any metric onMM)(1.2)∀x∈M,∀v∈ES​Ms,‖dx​φt​(v)‖φt​(x)≤C​e−ν​t​‖v‖x,ift>0,\displaystyle\forall x\in M,\forall v\in E^{s}_{SM},\|d_{x}\varphi_{t}(v)\|_{\varphi_{t}(x)}\leq Ce^{-\nu t}\|v\|_{x},\,\text{ if $t>0$},∀x∈M,∀v∈ES​Mu,‖dx​φt​(v)‖φt​(x)≤C​eν​t​‖v‖x,ift<0.\displaystyle\forall x\in M,\forall v\in E^{u}_{SM},\|d_{x}\varphi_{t}(v)\|_{\varphi_{t}(x)}\leq Ce^{\nu t}\|v\|_{x},\,\,\,\,\text{ if $t<0$}.

Of particular importance is the case of the geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of a manifold with a negative sectional curvature everywhere[Ano67]. The Anosov property is of central importance in the realm of dynamical systems: the central directionℝ​X\mathbb{R}X, not present for Anosov diffeomorphisms, makes the study of theirspeed of mixingvery challenging. When such flows exhibit thecontact property, much more can be said.

## Definition 1.1.

An Anosov flow generated by a vector fieldXXis said to be contact if there exists a smooth11-formα\alpha, defined byα​(X)=1\alpha(X)=1andker⁡(α)=Eu⊕Es\ker(\alpha)=E^{u}\oplus E^{s}, satisfying thatd​α|Eu⊕Esd\alpha_{|E^{u}\oplus E^{s}}is non-degenerate.

A celebrated result is the exponential mixing of contact Anosov flows with respect to the Liouville formα∧(d​α)n−1\alpha\wedge(d\alpha)^{n-1}(ifdim(M)=2​n−1\dim(M)=2n-1)[Dol98,Liv04,NZ14,FT24].
In particular, the geodesic flow is exponentially mixing on negatively curved manifolds with respect to the Liouville measure on their unit tangent bundles. The more recent approach in[NZ14,FT24]uses tools from microlocal and semiclassical analysis, whereas the one in[Dol98,Liv04]is more dynamically oriented and use of Markov partitions[Dol98]or Lasota-Yorke inequalities[Liv04]. In this paper, we will adopt the more recent point of view involving microlocal analysis.

The key ingredient to obtain the exponential mixing of(φt)(\varphi_{t})is to obtainresolvent estimatesfor the unbounded operatorXXon theanisotropic Sobolev spaceℋs​(S​M)\mathcal{H}^{s}(SM), a functional space adapted to the hyperbolic behaviour of the flow. Together with the spectral gap property, one obtains the exponential decay of correlations. In the context of smooth r-normally hyperbolic trapping, the work[Dya16]shows spectral gap and resolvent estimate properties for operators whose symbol has a Hamiltonian flow presenting a structure similar to the Anosov case. In fact, the ideas in[Dya16]apply to treat exponential mixing for contact Anosov flows such that their stable and unstable bundles are smooth. However the smoothness assumption is known to be very rigid, and only occur for Anosov flows built from the geodesic flow on hyperbolic manifold[HK90,BFL90].

In the recent paper[CG21], the authors were able to prove exponential mixing for contact Anosov flow in dimension33. In this caseEu,sE^{u,s}are known to beC1,αC^{1,\alpha}regular, which allows after some work to use the semiclassical proof of[Dya16]. The present work will assume smoothness ofEu,sE^{u,s}, but the flows under consideration will present additional(s) neutral directions: this is the setting ofpartially hyperbolic dynamics.

## Definition 1.2.

LetGGbe a compact Lie group andπ:P→M\pi:P\to MaGG-principal bundle whose right-multiplication byg∈Gg\in Gis writtenRgR_{g}. Let(φt)(\varphi_{t})be an Anosov flow onMM. A flow(Φt)(\Phi_{t})is said to be an isometric extension of(φt)(\varphi_{t})if the following conditions are fulfilled(1.3)π∘φt=Φt∘π,\displaystyle\pi\circ\varphi_{t}=\Phi_{t}\circ\pi,RgΦt(⋅)=Φt(Rg⋅),∀g∈G.\displaystyle R_{g}\Phi_{t}(\cdot)=\Phi_{t}(R_{g}\cdot),\,\,\forall g\in G.

Under suitable assumptions onPP,(Φt)(\Phi_{t})is known to be rapid mixing - the decay of correlation is faster than power any oftt- for measures which are locally the product of the Haar measure and an equilibrium state for the base flow by[PZ25]. The work[CL24]proved this with microlocal methods for volume preserving flow under more explicit geometric assumptions onPP(non-torsion ifdim(G)=1\dim(G)=1, semisimple otherwise), the base flow(φt)(\varphi_{t})(non-joint integrability type condition) and(Φt)(\Phi_{t})(ifdim(G)≥2\dim(G)\geq 2ergodicity is required).

In this paper, we will consider a specific case of isometric extensions: theframe flow over(Mn,g)(M^{n},g). WriteS​M:={(x,v)∈T​M|gx​(v,v)=1}SM:=\{(x,v)\in TM\,|\,g_{x}(v,v)=1\}and(φt)(\varphi_{t})the geodesic flow onS​MSM. On the orthonormal frame bundleF​M→S​MFM\to SM, we may define theframe flow(Φt)(\Phi_{t}). It corresponds to the parallel transport of a frame(x,v,𝐞1,…,𝐞n−1)(x,v,\mathbf{e}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{e}_{n-1})along the geodesic starting at(x,v)(x,v), see Section §3.1for precise definitions. This flow is anS​O​(n−1)SO(n-1)extension of the geodesic flow onS​MSM. We writeXF​MX_{FM}the generator of this flow. Now assume that(M,g)(M,g)is closed and has everywhere negative sectional curvature. Thanks to the fact that it satisfies Definition1.2withG=S​O​(n−1)G=SO(n-1), the frame flow is partially hyperbolic. We have the splitting(1.4)T​(F​M)=EF​Mu⊕EF​Ms⊕ℝ​XF​M⊕𝕍F​M,\displaystyle T(FM)=E^{u}_{FM}\oplus E^{s}_{FM}\oplus\mathbb{R}X_{FM}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{FM},

where thevertical bundle𝕍F​M\mathbb{V}_{FM}is tangent to the fiber direction ofF​M→S​MFM\to SMand is invariant byd​Φtd\Phi_{t}. The bundlesEF​Mu,sE^{u,s}_{FM}are also invariant subbundles additionally satisfying the usual contraction and expansion properties(1.5)∀p∈F​M,∀v∈EF​Ms,‖dx​Φt​(v)‖φt​(p)≤C​e−ν​t​‖v‖x,ift>0,\displaystyle\forall p\in FM,\forall v\in E^{s}_{FM},\|d_{x}\Phi_{t}(v)\|_{\varphi_{t}(p)}\leq Ce^{-\nu t}\|v\|_{x},\,\text{ if $t>0$},∀p∈F​M,∀v∈EF​Mu,‖dx​Φt​(v)‖φt​(x)≤C​eν​t​‖v‖x,ift<0.\displaystyle\forall p\in FM,\forall v\in E^{u}_{FM},\|d_{x}\Phi_{t}(v)\|_{\varphi_{t}(x)}\leq Ce^{\nu t}\|v\|_{x},\,\,\,\,\text{ if $t<0$}.

Since the work[BG80], much effort has been made to understand the dynamical property of this flow under the negative curvature assumption onMM, see[CLMS24a,CLMS24b]. For instance, it is known since[Moo]that it is exponentially mixing for the natural volume measure ifMMis an hyperbolic manifold, however the proof relies heavily on representation theory.

In[DGH21], Guillarmou and Delarue were able to incorporate the microlocal toolbox from[FS11]in the setting of the frame flow on hyperbolic 3-manifolds. They were able to prove exponential mixing for the normalized volume using those techniques. Note thatF​M→S​MFM\to SMis a circle bundle ifn=3n=3, this allows the use of a fiberwise Fourier decomposition of elements in𝒟′​(F​M)\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(FM), namely distributions onF​MFM. The spectral gap property for Anosov flows then becomes a gap which is uniform in the Fourier parameter. Higher dimensions seemed more difficult to tackle due to the fact thatF​M→S​MFM\to SMis in general aS​O​(n−1)SO(n-1)-principal bundle. Thanks to the recent important work[CL24], Cekić and Lefeuvre were able to construct the so-calledBorel-Weil calculus. This is a (semiclassical) pseudodifferential calculus that is adapted toGG-equivariant pseudodifferential operators onF​MFM,XF​MX_{FM}fits in this class. To such operator we may associate a family of operators acting on specific line bundles. We will heavily use the Borel-Weil calculus of[CL24]to tackle the case of higher-dimensional hyperbolic manifold. Different approaches using Dolgopyat’s inequality led to the proof of exponential mixing of the frame flow outside of the compact case on hyperbolic manifolds[SW21,LP23,LPS26].

We writeℕ=ℤ≥0\mathbb{N}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Those spaces have been introduced in[DGH21,CL24], they generalize the anisotropic spacesℋs\mathcal{H}^{s}of[FS11]tailored to Anosov flow in the case of principal bundle extensions: the additionalm∈ℝm\in\mathbb{R}parameter measure Sobolev regularity in the direction of𝕍F​M\mathbb{V}_{FM}.

Theorem1.3will be a consequence of the key technical estimate in Theorem4.8. We remark that the estimate (1.9) may be far from optimal, we did not try to sharpen it further. For instance, a consequence of the work[FT24, Theorem 1.7]is that the generator of a contact Anosov flow has uniformly bounded resolvent away from vertical lines of resonances. We believe that the bound (1.9) could be upgraded to a uniform bound using the techniques in[FT24]. We further think that the resolvent(−XF​M−z)−1(-X_{FM}-z)^{-1}could even be inℒ​(ℋm,s)\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{m,s}): themmparameter represents a Fourier parameter in the direction of the fiber ofF​M→S​MFM\to SM, it could be that the tools in[FT24]applies here to show that the resolvent does not downgrade the regularity in this direction.

A consequence of the resolvent estimate joint with the spectral gap property in Theorem1.3is the exponential mixing of the frame flow for the natural normalized volume onF​MFM.

## Corollary 1.4.

LetμF​M\mu_{FM}be the natural smooth volume onF​MFMwhich writes locally as a product of the Haar measure onS​O​(n−1)SO(n-1)and of the Liouville measure onS​MSM. Then the frame flow(Φt)(\Phi_{t})is exponentially mixing, there existsγ>0\gamma>0such that for all smoothf,gf,gthere existsCf,g>0C_{f,g}>0verifying(1.10)|∫(f∘Φ−t)​g​𝑑μF​M−∫f​𝑑μF​M​∫g​𝑑μF​M|≤Cf,g​e−γ​t\displaystyle\biggl|\int(f\circ\Phi_{-t})g\,\,d\mu_{FM}-\int fd\mu_{FM}\int gd\mu_{FM}\biggr|\leq C_{f,g}e^{-\gamma t}

For a proof that Corollary1.4implies Theorem1.3, the interested reader may refer to[DGH21, §4.5]where the this is done in the three-dimensional case: having at hand the estimate (1.9) and the spectral gap property, their proof applies in our setting.

We now make an important remark.

## Remark 1.5.

The rate of decayγ\gammais unknown here. From the quantum-classical correspondence in[DFG15], and following the arguments in[DGH21]in dimension 3,γ\gammashould be explicitly given in term of the first non-trivial eigenvalue of the positive scalar LaplacianΔ\DeltaonMM. Although interesting, we chose not to prove this. Outside of the locally symmetric case this correspondence is unknown, and we want this paper to be as self-contained as possible.

The strategy.In order to prove Theorem1.3, we will perform a Fourier decomposition of the generatorXF​MX_{FM}. In the three-dimensional case[DGH21], this gives a family of first order differential operatorsXkX_{k}on line bundlesL⊗kL^{\otimes k}overS​MSMwithk∈ℤk\in\mathbb{Z}. In higher dimensions, we use the Borel-Weil calculus of[CL24]to obtain a family of connections𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}over line bundles𝐋𝐤\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}over theflag bundleF→S​MF\to SM. We wish to estimate the norm of(−XF​M−z)−1(-X_{FM}-z)^{-1}on the spacesℋm,s​(F​M)\mathcal{H}^{m,s}(FM), see Definition4.7. It thus boils down to an estimate on the norm of each component(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}, well-defined by the calculus of Cekić-Lefeuvre[CL24]and[FS11]. The technical estimate that is needed is in Theorem1.3. To do so, the reasoning goes as follows.
- •

One first proves the estimate on{ℜ⁡(z)>−1}\{\Re(z)>-1\}. Assuming the estimate (4.7) does not hold, we obtain a sequence of quasimodes(uh)∈ℋhN​(F,𝐋𝐤​(h))(u_{h})\in\mathcal{H}^{N}_{h}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}(h)})and of complex numbers(zh)(z_{h})satisfying(1.11)(−h​𝐗𝐤​(h)−zh)​uh=o​(h2)\displaystyle(-h\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}(h)}-z_{h})u_{h}=o(h^{2})

wherehhis either1/⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩1/\langle\Im(z)\rangleor⟨𝐤⟩−1\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle^{-1}: this is thehigh-frequency regimeorhigh tensor power regimedichotomy.
- •

Ash→0h\to 0, we then study the microlocal properties of(uh)(u_{h}). As done in[Dya16],[CG21]or[DGH21], we study semiclassical measuresμ\muassociated to(uh)(u_{h}). We introduce them in the setting of the Borel-Weil calculus in Section2. Thanks to the fact that we assumedℜ⁡(zh)/h>−1\Re(z_{h})/h>-1, we are able to prove ahorocyclic invarianceofuhu_{h}, see Proposition5.10. We then deduce from there aLipschitz-boundsonμ\mu, see Lemma5.17.
- •

In the course of the proof, we introduce particular unstable and stable vector fields whose corresponding Hamiltonian vector fields aretransversal to the trapped setregion whereuhu_{h}microlocalizes. Due to the presence of the line bundles𝐋𝐤\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}, the underlyingclassical dynamicsin the Borel-Weil calculus may not agree with the one originating from the standard symplectic form. We thus have to be more careful than in the pure contact flow case.
- •

Combining the previous result gives a contradiction concerning the existence of the quasimodesuhu_{h};μ\muhas to simultaneously satisfy contradictory estimates, namely the Lipschitz bound and the propagation property with respect to the Hamiltonian flow of𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}.
- •

Using thehorocyclic operatorsintroduced in (3.1.2), we are able to deduce resolvent estimates everywhere away fromε\varepsilon-neighborhoods of vertical band{ℜ⁡(z)=1−n2−k}\{\Re(z)=\frac{1-n}{2}-k\}through an induction procedure onkk.

Perspectives.It is not clear for the moment if we should expect thatz↦(−XF​M−z)−1z\mapsto(-X_{FM}-z)^{-1}is meromorphic on the vertical lines{ℜ⁡(z)=1−n2−k,k∈ℕ}\{\Re(z)=\frac{1-n}{2}-k,k\in\mathbb{N}\}. This issue appears in[DGH21]and has been reformulated in terms of the spectral theory of the Bochner LaplacianΔN\Delta_{N}on the space of traceless symmetric tensors⨂S,0NT∗​M\bigotimes^{N}_{S,0}T^{*}M, using the quantum-classical correspondence. One has to be able to have a precise asymptotics forλ1​(ΔN)\lambda_{1}(\Delta_{N}). However in general there is no reason to expect meromorphic continuation. Simply consider the trivial extension of an Anosov flow,0will be a pole of infinite order. The ergodic properties of the frame flow may be a key property allowing to have meromorphic continuation.

Another perspective is to find a suitable way to study the spectrum of−XF​M+V-X_{FM}+VwhereV∈Cα​(F​M)V\in C^{\alpha}(FM). IfVVis constant on each fibers ofF​MFMthen the Fourier decomposition reasoning may work, up to incorporate the approach of[GdP22]to tackle the Hölder regularity. This may be interesting in order to understand the speed of mixing for equilibrium states which are locally product of the Haar measure and an equilibrium state of the geodesic flow. But ifVVhas non-trivial Fourier components, defining resonances does not seem trivial and it seems to be an interesting problem to address. It could potentially lead to the construction of other interesting equilibrium states for the frame flow by following the ideas of[Hum25]in the case of transitive Anosov flows.
Finally, the exponential mixing of frame flows on manifolds with strictly14\frac{1}{4}-pinched negative curvature still seems out of reach. In this work, the smoothness ofEu,sE^{u,s}was crucial. Although the approach of[CG21]could possibly tackle the case whereEF​Mu,sE^{u,s}_{FM}are onlyC1+εC^{1+\varepsilon}, the works[BCG95]and[Bea25]proved that this implies smoothness of the latter bundles. The fact that the curvature of the dynamical connectiond​Θd​y​nd\Theta_{dyn}onF​M→S​MFM\to SMis non-degenerate onEF​Mu⊕EF​MsE^{u}_{FM}\oplus E^{s}_{FM}, together with the contact property of the base flow(φt)(\varphi_{t}), seems however to be a key mechanism for exponential mixing (see Section3for the notations).

## 2.Analytic preliminaries: the Borel-Weil calculus

We provide a necessary overview of the construction in[CL24]of the Borel-Weil calculus. Then, we define semiclassical measures in this setting.

## 2.1.Representations ofS​O​(n)SO(n)and the Borel-Weil theorem

LetGGbe a compact connectedsemisimpleLie group of dimensionmm. ChooseTTa maximal torus ofGG(𝔱\mathfrak{t}the corresponding Lie subalgebra of𝔤\mathfrak{g}), that is a maximal compact connected Abelian subgroup. The dimension ofTTas a submanifold ofGGis called the rank and is writtendd.

Equivalence classes of irreducible unitary representations ofGG, whose set is writtenG^\widehat{G}, can be labeled by a subset of(i​𝔱)∗(i\mathfrak{t})^{*}(linear forms𝔱→i​ℝ\mathfrak{t}\to i\mathbb{R}). Elements of this set are the so-calledhighest weightofGG. Precisely, from a choice of simple roots one may form the so-called positive Weyl chamber𝒲+⊂(i​𝔱)∗\mathcal{W}_{+}\subset(i\mathfrak{t})^{*}. Choose a family of analytically integrals weights (i.e.elements of(i​𝔱)∗(i\mathfrak{t})^{*}that descend to a characterT→ℂT\to\mathbb{C}through the exponential map), denoted(λi)1≤i≤d∈(i​𝔱∗)d(\lambda_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}\in(i\mathfrak{t}^{*})^{d}, that generates analytically integral weights in𝒲+\mathcal{W}_{+}. Then we can construct the following labeling(2.1)ϕ​(𝐤):=∑i=1dki​λi,𝐤∈ℤ+d.\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{k}):=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\lambda_{i},\,\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}.

Define an equivalence relation onℤ+d\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}by declaring that𝐤∼𝐤′\mathbf{k}\sim\mathbf{k^{\prime}}ifϕ​(𝐤)=ϕ​(𝐤′)\phi(\mathbf{k})=\phi(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}). The set of corresponding equivalence classes is writtenΛ\Lambdaand can be seen to index complex irreducible representations ofGG.
As this will be useful in Lemma5.8, let us specify this in the case ofS​O​(n)SO(n)forn≥3n\geq 3. and forn=2​dn=2dωj\displaystyle\omega_{j}=i​(e1+⋯+ej),1≤j≤d−2\displaystyle=i(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{j}),\,1\leq j\leq d-2(2.3)ωd−1\displaystyle\omega_{d-1}=i​(e1+⋯+ed−1−ed),\displaystyle=i(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{d-1}-e_{d}),ωd\displaystyle\omega_{d}=i​(e1+⋯+ed−1+ed).\displaystyle=i(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{d-1}+e_{d}).

The followig result is standard, and shows thatΛ=ℤ+d\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d}for the caseG=S​O​(n)G=SO(n).

## Proposition 2.1.

The finite-dimensional irreducible complex representations ofS​O​(n)SO(n)have highest weightλ=∑i=1dmi​ωi\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{d}m_{i}\omega_{i}mi∈ℤ≥0m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}withmi∈ℤ+m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

## Proof.

This is a standard result, see for instance[FH91, §19.2, §19.4].

## 2.1.1.The Borel-Weil correspondence.

This theorem establishes an isomorphism between every complex irreducible representations ofGG, indexed by𝐤\mathbf{k}, and the corresponding complex canonical representation ofGG(called the Borel-Weil representation). The latter can be described as follow. To𝐤∈Λ\mathbf{k}\in\Lambdacan be associated an analytically integral weightα𝐤∈(i​𝔱)∗\alpha_{\mathbf{k}}\in(i\mathfrak{t})^{*}. It has the property of descending through the exponential map to a characterγ𝐤:T→𝕊1\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}:T\to\mathbb{S}^{1}. Thus, one can form the associated line bundle(2.4)𝐉𝐤:=G×γ𝐤ℂ,\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}:=G\times_{\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}}\mathbb{C},

by the quotient ofG×ℂG\times\mathbb{C}by[g,z]∼[g⋅t,γ𝐤​(t)−1​z][g,z]\sim[g\cdot t,\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}(t)^{-1}z]fort∈Tt\in T. It can be shown thatG/TG/Thas a holomorphic structure with respect to which𝐉𝐤\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}is a holomorphic line bundle, see[Sep07]. It can be endowed with the canonicalGG-action given by left-translationg⋅[h​T,z]=[g​h​T,z]g\cdot[hT,z]=[ghT,z]. The Borel-Weil representation is formed by the data of the finite-dimensional vector space of holomorphic sections of𝐉𝐤\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}writtenH0​(G/T,𝐉γ𝐤)H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}})and of the mapping(2.5)Φ​(g)​s​(h​T):=g⋅s​(g−1​h​T).\displaystyle\Phi(g)s\,(hT):=g\cdot s(g^{-1}hT).

## Remark 2.2.

A sections∈H0​(G/T,𝐉γ𝐤)s\in H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}})equivariantly lifts to a functions¯∈C∞​(G,ℂ)\overline{s}\in C^{\infty}(G,\mathbb{C})verifyings¯​(g⋅t)=γ𝐤​(t)−1​s¯​(g)\overline{s}(g\cdot t)=\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}(t)^{-1}\overline{s}(g). Indeed, just defines¯\overline{s}bys​(h​T)=[h,s¯​(h)]s(hT)=[h,\overline{s}(h)]. At the level of lifted sections, the previous representation readsΦ¯​(g)​s¯​(g0):=s¯​(g−1​g0)\overline{\Phi}(g)\overline{s}\,(g_{0}):=\overline{s}(g^{-1}g_{0}).

For a representationVV, writeV∗V^{*}the corresponding dual representation.

## Theorem 2.3.

Letλ​(γ)\lambda(\gamma)be an analytically integral weight whose corresponding character isγ\gammaand define𝐉−λ​(γ)\mathbf{J}^{-\lambda(\gamma)}the corresponding associated bundle (2.4) usingγ−1\gamma^{-1}. If−λ​(γ)∉𝒲+-\lambda(\gamma)\notin\mathcal{W}^{+}, thenH0​(G/T,𝐉−λ​(γ))={0}H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{-\lambda(\gamma)})=\{0\}. If not, letV−λ​(γ)V^{-\lambda(\gamma)}be the unique complex irreducible representation ofGGwith highest weight−λ​(γ)∈𝒲+-\lambda(\gamma)\in\mathcal{W}^{+}. There is the following isomorphism of representations(2.6)H0​(G/T,𝐉−λ​(γ))≅(V−λ​(γ))∗,\displaystyle H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{-\lambda(\gamma)})\cong(V^{-\lambda(\gamma)})^{*},

## Proof.

For more information and the proof of the theorem, we refer to[Sep07, Lemma 7.5]. .
∎

As an example, the reader may bear in mind theS​O​(3)SO(3)case. Real irreducible representations are indexed by odd integers2​l+12l+1(l≥0l\geq 0) and correspond to the usual representation on spherical harmonics𝐇2​l+1:={P∈ℝl​[x1,x2,x3],P​(λ​x)=λl​P​(x),Δ​P=0}\mathbf{H}_{2l+1}:=\{P\in\mathbb{R}_{l}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}],\,P(\lambda x)=\lambda^{l}P(x)\,\,,\Delta P=0\}. The corresponding complexified representation is still irreducible. Doing this,𝐇2​l+1⊗ℂ\mathbf{H}_{2l+1}\otimes\mathbb{C}actually identifies withH0​(ℂ​ℙ1,𝒪​(2​l))H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathcal{O}(2l))and thus provides a concrete manifestation of the previous theorem. Here𝒪​(2​l)\mathcal{O}(2l)is𝒪​(1)⊗2​l\mathcal{O}(1)^{\otimes{2l}}where𝒪​(1)\mathcal{O}(1)is the dual to the tautological bundle onℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}.

The chosen system of roots spanning irreducible representation(λi)1≤i≤d∈(i​𝔱∗)d(\lambda_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}\in(i\mathfrak{t}^{*})^{d}allows one to construct individual line bundles(J1,…,Jd)(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{d}). We will write𝐉𝐤=⨂1≤i≤dJi⊗ki\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}=\bigotimes_{1\leq i\leq d}J^{\otimes k_{i}}_{i}with𝐤:=(ki)1≤i≤d\mathbf{k}:=(k_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}. The metricg𝐤g^{\mathbf{k}}will stand for the induced metric on𝐉𝐤\mathbf{J}^{{\mathbf{k}}}by the associated bundle construction. The bi-invariant metric onGGinduces a metric onG/TG/T, hence a Riemannian volume writtend​μTd\mu_{T}. This allows us to define a scalar product onL2​(G/T,𝐉γ𝐤)L^{2}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}}).

Finally, the data of the metricg𝐤g^{\mathbf{k}}on𝐉𝐤\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}uniquely defines the associated Chern connection∇𝐉𝐤\nabla_{\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}}, whose(0,1)(0,1)-part will be written∂¯𝐉𝐤\overline{\partial}_{\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}}. We thus have by definition(2.7)H0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤):=ker⁡(∂¯𝐉𝐤).\displaystyle H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}):=\ker\bigl(\,\overline{\partial}_{\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}}\bigr).

The latter family is easier to manipulate than a family of sections of vector bundles whose rank may even go to infinity.

LetTTbe a maximal torus ofGG. We can form the fiber bundlesF:=P/T→MF:=P/T\to Mwhose typical fiber is the flag manifoldG/TG/T. Each fiber possesses a holomorphic structure.

Also defineH0​(F,𝐋𝐤):=∐x∈MH0​(Fx,𝐋|Fx𝐤),\displaystyle H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}):=\coprod_{x\in M}H^{0}(F_{x},\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}_{|F_{x}}),

which is a smooth vector bundle over M.

Fromf∈C∞​(P)f\in C^{\infty}(P)one obtains the family of sections(ℱ​f​(⋅,λ))λ∈G^(\mathcal{F}f(\cdot,\lambda))_{\lambda\in\hat{G}}. This seems to be a standard result, but since we did not manage to find a reference, we quickly prove the statement for completeness. Recall thatπ:P→M\pi:P\to Mis the bundle projection. To eachp∈Pp\in Pwe can associateψp:G→Px\psi_{p}:G\to P_{x}the canonical isomorphismg→p⋅gg\to p\cdot g. We can form the following mapP×H0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤)\displaystyle P\times H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})⟶H0​(F,𝐋𝐤)\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})Ψ:(p,s)\displaystyle\Psi\colon\quad\quad\quad(p,s)⟼ΨT−1​((ψp)∗​s¯)\displaystyle\longmapsto\Psi_{T}^{-1}\bigl((\psi_{p})_{*}\overline{s}\bigr)

wheres¯∈C∞​(G,ℂ)\overline{s}\in C^{\infty}(G,\mathbb{C})is theTT-equivariant lift ofss, which descends to an holomorphic section of𝐉𝐤→G/T\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}}\to G/T. For anyp′:=p⋅g′∈Pxp^{\prime}:=p\cdot g^{\prime}\in P_{x}s¯​(ψp−1​(p′⋅t))\displaystyle\overline{s}(\psi_{p}^{-1}(p^{\prime}\cdot t))=s¯​(g′⋅t)\displaystyle=\overline{s}(g^{\prime}\cdot t)=γ𝐤​(t−1)​s¯​(ψp−1​(p′)),\displaystyle=\gamma_{\mathbf{k}}(t^{-1})\overline{s}(\psi_{p}^{-1}(p^{\prime})),

thus descending to a section𝐋𝐤→Fπ​(p)\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\to F_{\pi(p)}thanks toΨT−1\Psi_{T}^{-1}.Ψ\Psidescend to the associated bundle; for anyg,g′∈Gg,g^{\prime}\in G(p′:=p⋅g′p^{\prime}:=p\cdot g^{\prime})Ψ​(p⋅g,Φ​(g−1)​s)\displaystyle\Psi(p\cdot g,\Phi(g^{-1})s)=ΨT−1​(Φ​(g−1)​s¯​(ψp⋅g−1​(p⋅g′)))\displaystyle=\Psi_{T}^{-1}\bigl(\overline{\Phi(g^{-1})s}(\psi_{p\cdot g}^{-1}(p\cdot g^{\prime}))\bigr)=ΨT−1​(s¯​(g​ψp⋅g​((p⋅g)⋅g−1​g′)))\displaystyle=\Psi_{T}^{-1}\bigl(\overline{s}(g\,\psi_{p\cdot g}((p\cdot g)\cdot g^{-1}g^{\prime}))\bigr)=ΨT−1​(s¯​(ψp−1​(p′)))\displaystyle=\Psi_{T}^{-1}\bigl(\overline{s}(\psi_{p}^{-1}(p^{\prime}))\bigr)=Ψ​(p,s),\displaystyle=\Psi(p,s),

where we used Remark2.2in the second equality and the relationψp−1​(p′)=g′\psi_{p}^{-1}(p^{\prime})=g^{\prime}. We thus get the desired smooth isomorphism (holomorphicity is easily checked).

∎

This isomorphism induces an isomorphism of vector bundlesEλ≅H0​(F,𝐋𝐤)E^{\lambda}\cong H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})where𝐤=(ki)1≤i≤d\mathbf{k}=(k_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}is such thatλ=∑ki​λi\lambda=\sum k_{i}\lambda_{i}. To account for multiplicity of an irreducible representation in the Fourier decoposition off∈C∞​(P)f\in C^{\infty}(P), we writed𝐤:=dim(H0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤))d_{\mathbf{k}}:=\dim(H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})).

Due to the fiberwise Fourier transform and Borel-Weil theory, we were able to pass data from a smooth functionf∈C∞​(P)f\in C^{\infty}(P)to a family(f𝐤,i)𝐤∈Λ,1≤i≤d𝐤(f_{\mathbf{k},i})_{\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda,1\leq i\leq d_{\mathbf{k}}}of fiberwise holomorphic sections of the line bundle𝐋𝐤\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}. For simplicity the multiplicity index in the Fourier decomposition will be omitted most of the time since it will not impact the arguments of the next sections.

To finish, suppose that we equipP→MP\to Mwith a connectionℍP\mathbb{H}_{P}. This connection induces a canonical connectionℍF\mathbb{H}_{F}on the homogeneousGG-bundleF→MF\to M. The complexified Lie algebra𝔤ℂ\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}ofGGsplits according to(2.11)𝔤ℂ=𝔱ℂ⊕𝔫+⊕𝔫−,\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-},

with𝔫±\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}being the positive and negative root spaces for the adjoint representation of𝔤\mathfrak{g}. For that purpose we will write for a vector fieldXXonMM(resp.FF) its horizontal liftXℍFX^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}toFFandXℍPX^{\mathbb{H}_{P}}toP→MP\to M(resp.XℍP→FX^{\mathbb{H}_{P\to F}}its lift toP→FP\to F).

For the horizontal connection∇𝐤:C∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)→C∞​(F,𝐋𝐤⊗ℍF∗)\nabla^{\mathbf{k}}:C^{\infty}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})\to C^{\infty}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}), we define froms∈Chol∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)s\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})andX∈C∞​(M,T​M)X\in C^{\infty}(M,TM)(2.12)∇XℍF𝐤s¯:=XℍP​s¯,\displaystyle\overline{\nabla^{\mathbf{k}}_{X^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}}s}:=X^{\mathbb{H}_{P}}\overline{s},

which by construction descends to a section written∇XℍF𝐤s\nabla^{\mathbf{k}}_{X^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}}s.

For the full connection∇¯𝐤:C∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)→C∞​(F,𝐋𝐤⊗T∗​F)\overline{\nabla}^{\mathbf{k}}:C^{\infty}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})\to C^{\infty}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes T^{*}F), we define froms∈Chol∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)s\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})andX∈Γ​(T​F)X\in\Gamma(TF)(2.13)∇¯X𝐤​s¯:=XℍP→F​s¯,\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\nabla}_{X}^{\mathbf{k}}s}:=X^{\mathbb{H}_{P\to F}}\overline{s},

the induced section overFFwill be written∇¯X𝐤​s\overline{\nabla}^{\mathbf{k}}_{X}s.

## Proposition 2.6.

The complexified horizontal spaceℍF⊗ℂ:=ℍF​(ℂ)\mathbb{H}_{F}\otimes\mathbb{C}:=\mathbb{H}_{F}(\mathbb{C})and its dual can be equipped with a canonical fiberwise complex structure. Moreover,[∇𝐤,Π𝐤]:=Π𝐤ℍF​(ℂ)∗​∇𝐤−∇𝐤Π𝐤=0[\nabla^{\mathbf{k}},\Pi_{{\mathbf{k}}}]:=\Pi_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathbb{H}_{F}(\mathbb{C})^{*}}\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}-\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}\Pi_{\mathbf{k}}=0whereΠ𝐤ℍF​(ℂ)∗:L2​(F,𝐋𝐤⊗ℍF​(ℂ)∗)→Lhol2​(F,𝐋𝐤⊗ℍF​(ℂ)∗)\Pi^{\mathbb{H}_{F}(\mathbb{C})^{*}}_{\mathbf{k}}:L^{2}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes\mathbb{H}_{F}(\mathbb{C})^{*})\to L^{2}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes\mathbb{H}_{F}(\mathbb{C})^{*})is the orthogonal projection on fiberwise holomorphic sections.

## 2.2.Uniform calculus on𝐋𝐤→F\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\to F

For an in-depth treatment of the following concepts, we again refer to[CL24, §3]. We wish to provide the reader with the tools that we will use in order to prove our main result. Only in this sub-section willFFstand for any closed manifold: we don’t need the fiberwise holomorphic structure to define the uniform calculus on𝐋𝐤→F\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\to F. We fixL1,..,LdL_{1},..,L_{d}to be complex Hermitian line bundles onFFand∇=(∇1,…,∇d)\nabla=(\nabla_{1},\dotsc,\nabla_{d})a family of unitary connections on𝐋:=(L1,..,Ld)\mathbf{L}:=(L_{1},..,{L}_{d}). In this section only,Λ\Lambdais any subset ofℤd\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

Leth∈(0,1]h\in(0,1]and introduceΩ​(h):={𝐤∈Λ,h​⟨𝐤⟩≤1}\Omega(h):=\bigl\{\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda,\,h\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle\leq 1\bigr\}. Notice here that we can construct sequences of elements inΩ​(h)\Omega(h)such that(𝐤​(h))h(\mathbf{k}(h))_{h}has a norm that goes to infinity ashhgoes to0. We wish to construct semiclassical pseudodifferential operators acting on sections of𝐋𝐤→F\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\to F, and we additionally want to take into account the parameter𝐤\mathbf{k}.

First, to take into account the parameter𝐤\mathbf{k}we define theuniform class of semiclassical pseudodifferential operators onFF,Ψh,𝐤∙​(F)\Psi^{\bullet}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(F).

## Definition 2.7.

Given a covering(Ui,φi)(U_{i},\varphi_{i})ofFFby charts, we say that a smooth functionah,𝐤a_{h,\mathbf{k}}onT∗​MT^{*}Mis in the uniform classSh,𝐤m​(T∗​F)S^{m}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(T^{*}F)if, for anyii,h∈(0,1]h\in(0,1]and𝐤∈Ω​(h)\mathbf{k}\in\Omega(h)|∂ξβ∂xα(ah,𝐤∘d​φi⊤)​(x,ξ)|≤Cα,β,i​⟨ξ⟩m−|β|,(x,ξ)∈T∗​Ui,\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(a_{h,\mathbf{k}}\circ d\varphi_{i}^{\top}\,)(x,\xi)\bigr|\leq C_{\alpha,\beta,i}\langle\xi\rangle^{m-|\beta|},\,(x,\xi)\in T^{*}U_{i},

Notice that the bound is uniform both inhhand𝐤\mathbf{k}. The corresponding class of (semiclassical) pseudodifferential operators isΨh,𝐤m​(F):={Oph⁡(a)+R,R∈h∞​Ψh,𝐤−∞​(F)},\displaystyle\Psi^{m}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(F):=\bigl\{\operatorname{Op}_{h}(a)+R,\,R\in h^{\infty}\Psi_{h,\mathbf{k}}^{-\infty}(F)\bigr\},

whereOph\operatorname{Op}_{h}is a quantization procedure (see[DZ19, Appendix E]), elements inΨh,𝐤−∞​(F)\Psi_{h,\mathbf{k}}^{-\infty}(F)are operators with smooth Schwartz kernel having each of itsC∞C^{\infty}seminorms uniformly bounded inhhand𝐤∈Ω​(h)\mathbf{k}\in\Omega(h).

The reader should bear in mind the following important remark. It shows the important difference with the usual semiclassical calculus.

## Remark 2.8.

The connectionh​∇𝐤h{\nabla}^{\mathbf{k}}takes the formi​h​d+i​h​𝐤⋅βihd+ih\mathbf{k}\cdot\betain local coordinates with respect to a local unit sectionsk1⊗⋯⊗skds^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes s^{k_{d}}(β:=(∇isi/si)1≤i≤d\beta:=(\nabla_{i}s_{i}/s_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}). This motivates the following definition

## Definition 2.9.

The principal symbolσh,𝐤​(h)​(A)\sigma_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}(A)ofA∈Ψh,𝐤​(F)A\in\Psi_{h,\mathbf{k}}(F)with respect to the functionh↦𝐤​(h)h\mapsto\mathbf{k}(h)is defined as the principal symbol of the operatorAh,𝐤​(h)∈Ψh​(F)A_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}\in\Psi_{h}(F).
In turn,σA\sigma_{A}can be seen as a function taking values in functionsh↦𝐤​(h)h\mapsto\mathbf{k}(h)and returningσAh,k​(h)\sigma_{A_{h,k(h)}}.

In semiclassical analysis it is usual to work with thefiber-radially compactified cotangent bundleT∗​F¯\overline{T^{*}F}, see[DZ19, §E.1.3]for a precise definition. Roughly speaking, its interior is diffeomorphic toT∗​FT^{*}Fwhile its boundary is diffeomorphic to the cosphere bundleS∗​FS^{*}F. We can define forA∈Ψh,𝐤​(F)A\in\Psi_{h,\mathbf{k}}(F)its elliptic setell⁡(A)\operatorname{ell}(A)and its wavefront setWF​(A)\text{WF}(A)as(2.14)ellh⁡(A)\displaystyle\operatorname{ell}_{h}(A):=⋂h↦𝐤​(h)ell⁡(Ah,𝐤​(h))⊆T∗​F¯,\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{h\mapsto\mathbf{k}(h)}\operatorname{ell}(A_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)})\subseteq\overline{T^{*}F},(2.15)WFh​(A)\displaystyle\text{WF}_{h}(A):=⋃h↦𝐤​(h)WF​(Ah,𝐤​(h))⊆T∗​F¯.\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{h\mapsto\mathbf{k}(h)}\text{WF}(A_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)})\subseteq\overline{T^{*}F}.

The usual results on the inversion of microelliptic operators carry on in this calculus.
Building on this calculus, we now define the classΨh∙​(F,𝐋)\Psi^{\bullet}_{h}(F,\mathbf{L})of[CL24, §3.2].

## 2.2.1.Propagation properties in the uniform calculus

An important consequence of the definition of the principal symbol in the semiclassical uniform calculus is the fact that the subsequent "classical dynamics" of an operatorOph,𝐤⁡(ah,𝐤)\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(a_{h,\mathbf{k}})is not simply the Hamiltonian flow ofaawith respect to the Liouville22-formω0\omega_{0}onT∗​FT^{*}F.

Write𝐅∇¯:=(F∇¯1,…,F∇¯d)∈C∞​(F,Λ2​T∗​F)d\mathbf{F}_{\overline{\nabla}}:=(F_{\overline{\nabla}_{1}},\dotsc,F_{\overline{\nabla}_{d}})\in C^{\infty}(F,\Lambda^{2}T^{*}F)^{d}the vector of curvature forms of eachLiL_{i}(each one of them is purely imaginary). We define the following22-form onT∗​FT^{*}F(2.20)ωh,𝐤:=ω0+i​h​𝐤⋅πT∗​F∗​𝐅∇¯.\displaystyle\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}:=\omega_{0}+ih\mathbf{k}\cdot\pi_{T^{*}F}^{*}\mathbf{F}_{\overline{\nabla}}.

Thanks to the Bianchi identity, this form is closed. A computation in local coordinates gives non-degeneracy ofωh,𝐤\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}[CL24, Lemma 3.2.10], making(T∗​F,ωh,𝐤)(T^{*}F,\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}})a symplectic manifold. For a smooth functionffonT∗​FT^{*}F, writeHfωh,𝐤H_{f}^{\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}}the corresponding Hamiltonian vector field and(Φtωh,𝐤,f)(\Phi^{\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}},f}_{t})its Hamiltonian flow. WritepX,pYp_{X},p_{Y}the usual Hamiltonian functions ofX,Y∈Γ​(T​F)X,Y\in\Gamma(TF):pX​(x,ξ)=ξ​(X​(x))p_{X}(x,\xi)=\xi(X(x)). We have the following useful identity forωh,𝐤\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}(we refer the reader to[CL24, 3.2.18])(2.21){pX,pY}ωh,𝐤=p[X,Y]+i​h​𝐤⋅𝐅∇¯​(X,Y),\displaystyle\{p_{X},p_{Y}\}^{\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}}=p_{[X,Y]}+ih\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{F}_{\overline{\nabla}}(X,Y),

where{⋅,⋅}ωh,𝐤\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}}is the Poisson bracket associated toωh,𝐤\omega_{h,\mathbf{k}}.

## 2.3.The Borel-Weil calculus.

FFand𝐋𝐤\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}are now as in Definition2.4. We were able to pass from the data of a smooth function onPPto the data of a family of Fourier modes, namely sections inChol∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}). More precisely, we associated to the data off∈C∞​(P)f\in C^{\infty}(P)the family of sectionsℱ​f:=(f𝐤,i)𝐤∈Λ,i≤d𝐤\mathcal{F}f:=(f_{\mathbf{k},i})_{\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda,i\leq d_{\mathbf{k}}},d𝐤d_{\mathbf{k}}being the dimension of the irreducible representation corresponding to the label𝐤∈Λ\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda. From this, the work done by Cekić-Lefeuvre allows to build a calculus such that the class ofGG-equivariant pseudodifferential operators onPPwould fit in[CL24, §3.3]. Such operatorsAAsatisfy by definitionA​(Rg∗​f)=Rg∗​A​(f),Rgright-multiplication byg.\displaystyle A(R_{g}^{*}f)=R_{g}^{*}A(f),\text{ $R_{g}$ right-multiplication by $g$}.

Those operators induce, by the usual Fourier decomposition and the Borel-Weil theorem, a family of operators𝐀𝐤:Chol∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)→Chol∞​(F,𝐋𝐤)\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{k}}:C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})\to C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}). For instance, a vector fieldX∈Γ​(ℍP)X\in\Gamma(\mathbb{H}_{P})satisfying(Rg)∗​X=X(R_{g})_{*}X=Xenjoys such a property (i.e.horizontal lifts of vector fields onMM). It induces a family of operators(𝐗𝐤)(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}), which is explicitly seen to be∇XℍF𝐤=∇¯XℍF𝐤\nabla^{\mathbf{k}}_{X^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}}=\overline{\nabla}^{\mathbf{k}}_{X^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}}(notice the absence of horizontal line over the connection symbol), this will be our main object of interest in the following sections.

Indeed, for𝐀:=Π∙​𝐀∙​Π∙∈Ψh,BW​(P)\mathbf{A}:=\Pi_{\bullet}\mathbf{A}_{\bullet}\Pi_{\bullet}\in\Psi_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(P), we have by admissibilityh​∂¯∙​𝐀∙∈𝒪​(h∞)​Ψ−∞\displaystyle h\overline{\partial}_{\bullet}\mathbf{A}_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})\Psi^{-\infty}

buth​∂¯𝐤∈Ψh,𝐤1​(F,𝐋𝐤)h\overline{\partial}_{\mathbf{k}}\in\Psi^{1}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})verifies thatell∇¯⁡(h​∂¯𝐤)=T∗​F¯∖ℍF∗¯\operatorname{ell}^{\overline{\nabla}}(h\overline{\partial}_{\mathbf{k}})=\overline{T^{*}F}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}, thusWF∇¯​(𝐀𝐤)⊆ℍF∗¯\text{WF}^{\overline{\nabla}}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{k}})\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{H}^{*}_{F}}for all𝐤\mathbf{k}by elliptic inversion in the uniform calculus.

## Remark 2.17.

Following the construction of anisotropic spaces for Anosov flows in[CL24, Chapter 9], it is tempting to try to carry this construction using the generator of the frame flowXF​MX_{FM}(we recall that the latter is not Anosov because of the additional neutral directions in (1.4)). The symplectic lift of the flow ofXF​MX_{FM}has the same dynamical properties as in[CL24, §9.1.2]for the Anosov case, replacingE0∗E_{0}^{*}byE0∗⊕𝕍F​M∗E_{0}^{*}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{FM}^{*}. The construction of the escape functionGmG_{m}carries on as in[CL24, Lemma 9.1.9]. However the proof of the meromorphic continuation property of the resolvent of an Anosov vector field uses crucially the ellipticity of theξ​(X)\xi(X)in the complementary directions ofEu∗⊕E0∗E_{u}^{*}\oplus E_{0}^{*}, a property which is not available forXF​MX_{FM}:ξ​(XF​M​(x))=0\xi(X_{FM}(x))=0for all(x,ξ)∈𝕍F​M∗(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{V}_{FM}^{*}. The main benefit of working with fiberwise holomorphic sections is to forget the co-vertical direction𝕍F​M∗\mathbb{V}_{FM}^{*}and only considerℍF∗\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}.

Before moving on to the next section, we briefly overview some important definition and properties of the calculus in the classΨh,BW\Psi_{h,\mathrm{BW}}, we again refer to[CL24, §3.3]for details and proofs.

First, in the spirit of the exponential map quantization, one can quantize symbols inSh,𝐤m​(ℍF∗)S^{m}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*})in the following way, see for more details[CL24, §3.3]. For two pointsx1,x2∈Mx_{1},x_{2}\in Mclose enough, writeγ\gammathe unique geodesic joining them andτγF\tau^{F}_{\gamma}the corresponding parallel transportFx1→Fx2F_{x_{1}}\to F_{x_{2}}with respect toℍF\mathbb{H}_{F}. We may now defineτγ𝐤\tau_{\gamma}^{\mathbf{k}}to be the parallel transport map on𝐋|Fx1𝐤\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}_{|F_{x_{1}}}with respect to∇¯𝐤\overline{\nabla}^{\mathbf{k}}alongτγF\tau_{\gamma}^{F}.

We refer the interested reader to[CL24, Lemma 3.3.20]for a proof that both quantization coincides up to a𝒪​(h)\mathcal{O}(h)remainder (in their proof, the variablez∈G/Tz\in G/Twill not appear and the claim is a mere application of the Kuranishi trick).

## Definition 2.20.

If𝐀=Π𝐤​𝐀′​Π𝐤=Π𝐤​𝐀~​Π𝐤\mathbf{A}=\Pi_{\mathbf{k}}\mathbf{A^{\prime}}\Pi_{\mathbf{k}}=\Pi_{\mathbf{k}}\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\Pi_{\mathbf{k}}we have thatσh,𝐤​(h)∇¯​(𝐀)|ℍF∗−σh,𝐤​(h)∇¯​(𝐀~)|ℍF∗=𝒪Shm−1​(h)\sigma^{\overline{\nabla}}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}(\mathbf{A})_{|\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}-\sigma^{\overline{\nabla}}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}})_{|\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}=\mathcal{O}_{S^{m-1}_{h}}(h)by[CL24, Lemma 3.3.14], giving sense to the previous definition.

From now on, if clear from the context, we will omit to mention the BW superscript and that the previous objects are computed with respect to∇¯\overline{\nabla}.

This proposition gathered the basic properties of the calculus inΨh,BW\Psi_{h,\text{BW}}. In Section5, we will extensively use additional properties of the calculus concerning propagation of singularities. This is the context of the following more advanced proposition. For the definitions ofradial sink and sources, we refer the reader to[DZ19, Appendix E]for the definitions or to §4.1for the specification to our setting. The Hamiltonian flow of an Anosov vector field presents a sink-source structure, see Section4. This is for instance achieved whenXXgenerates the geodesic flow on a closed hyperbolic manifold (this will be exactly the setting we will consider in the following sections).YYis called a basic vector field onFF. The threshold conditions are particularly simple in this case thanks to the volume preserving assumptions onXXand the fact that the contraction and expansion rate of the flow are equal to11. More general statement exists but they will not be used in this work.

In the version of Egorov theorem in the Borel-Weil calculus, we used that the horizontal liftYℍFY^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}onFFof any vector fieldYYonMMinduces an operator𝐘𝐤:=∇Y𝐤\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{k}}:=\nabla^{\mathbf{k}}_{Y}which is in the calculus. This is a consequence of Proposition2.6. In the rest of the paper, this is precisely the kind of operator that will be under consideration. Finally let us stress the following.

## Remark 2.23.

In the uniform calculus, we are free to choose a family of unitary connections to define the quantization. However, in the Borel-Weil calculus, the choice is made at the level of the principal bundle connectionℍ\mathbb{H}onP→MP\to M. The connections∇¯\overline{\nabla}under consideration are then naturally associated to this choice, see (2.13).

Lastly, we need the following functional spaces.

whereΔ𝐤:=∇𝐤∗∇𝐤\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}:=\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}, see (2.12).

The latter space can alternatively be seen to be equal toOph,BW⁡(⟨ξℍF∗⟩−s/2)​Lhol2​(F,𝐋𝐤)\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(\langle\xi_{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}\rangle^{-s/2})L^{2}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}), the principal symbol of the horizontal LaplacianΔ𝐤∈Ψh,BW2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\in\Psi^{2}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}being|ξℍF∗|gF2|\xi_{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}|^{2}_{g_{F}}[CL24, §3.3.2].

## Proof.

The proof is almost identical to that of[DZ19, E.44]in the scalar case. We quickly summarize the proof to emphasize the well-behaved properties of the Borel-Weil calculus. Consider(hk)(h_{k})such thathk→0h_{k}\to 0ask→+∞k\to+\inftyand writeΛh,N:=Oph,BW⁡(⟨ξℍF∗⟩−N)\Lambda_{h,N}:=\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(\langle\xi_{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}\rangle^{-N}).

Consider the following functionalIh​(a):=⟨Oph,BW⁡(a)​vh,vh⟩Lhol2​(F,𝐋𝐤​(h)),a∈Cc∞​(ℍF∗).\displaystyle I_{h}(a):=\langle\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(a)v_{h},v_{h}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}(h)})},\,a\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}).

On the other hand, one notices that by compositionOph,BW⁡(a)=Λh,N∗​Oph,BW⁡(a​⟨ξℍF∗⟩2​N)​Λh,N+𝒪Hh−N→HhN​(h).\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(a)=\Lambda_{h,N}^{*}\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(a\langle\xi_{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}\rangle^{2N})\Lambda_{h,N}\,+\,\mathcal{O}_{H_{h}^{-N}\to H_{h}^{N}}(h).

Using this relation, the assumption on(vh)(v_{h})andL2L^{2}-boundedness in the uniform calculus gives the following bound|Ih​(a)|\displaystyle|I_{h}(a)|≤‖Oph,BW⁡(a​⟨ξℍF∗⟩2​N)​Λh,N​vh‖L2​‖vh‖Hh,hol−N+𝒪​(h)\displaystyle\leq\|\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(a\langle\xi_{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}\rangle^{2N})\Lambda_{h,N}\,v_{h}\|_{L^{2}}\|v_{h}\|_{H^{-N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}}+\mathcal{O}(h)≤C2​sup(x,ξ)|⟨ξℍF∗⟩−N​a​(x,ξ)|+𝒪​(h)\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\sup_{(x,\xi)}|\langle\xi_{\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}}\rangle^{-N}a(x,\xi)|+\mathcal{O}(h)≲sup(x,ξ)|a​(x,ξ)|,\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{(x,\xi)}|a(x,\xi)|,

with an implicit constant depending onCCand on the support ofaa. In the second inequality, we use the Calderon-Vaillancourt inequality[CL24, Proposition 3.2.3]. Taking{al}\{a_{l}\}a dense family of test functions inCc​(ℍF∗)C_{c}(\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}), we can use the previous uniform bound for eachala_{l}to diagonally extract as in[DZ19, E.44]a subsequence(Ihj​(a))(I_{h_{j}}(a))which converges for every smoothaa. We defineI:Cc∞​(ℍF∗)→ℝI:C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*})\to\mathbb{R}by the previous limits. The uniform bound allows to extendIIto a continuous functional onCc0​(ℍF∗)C^{0}_{c}(\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}). The Riesz theorem gives the desired measure. That the latter is positive follows from the Gårding inequality in the BW calculus, which itself follows from the usual Gårding inequality, see[CL24, Lemma 3.3.16].
SinceIIis real valued for real valuedaa, we conclude. For simplicity, we do not continue to label the extracted sequence that allowed to obtainIhj​(a)→μ​(a)I_{h_{j}}(a)\to\mu(a).

For the support property, notice that for all𝐀∈Ψh,BWcomp\mathbf{A}\in\Psi^{\mathrm{comp}}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}chosen to be microsupported on{𝐩≠0}\{\mathbf{p}\neq 0\}there exists𝐐∈Ψh,BWcomp\mathbf{Q}\in\Psi^{\mathrm{comp}}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}such that𝐀=𝐐𝐏+𝐑\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{P}+\mathbf{R}with𝐑∈h∞​Ψh,BW−∞\mathbf{R}\in h^{\infty}\Psi^{-\infty}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}. Using the estimate onvhv_{h}⟨𝐀​vh,vh⟩\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{A}v_{h},v_{h}\rangle=⟨𝐐​(𝐏​vh),vh⟩+𝒪​(h∞)\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{P}v_{h}),v_{h}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})→0,\displaystyle\to 0,

by definition ofμ\muwe obtain∫σh,BW​(𝐀)​𝑑μ=0\int\sigma_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(\mathbf{A})d\mu=0. As a consequencesupp⁡(μ)⊆{𝐩=0}\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\bigl\{\mathbf{p}=0\bigr\}.

## 3.1.1.Frame bundle and frame flow on hyperbolic manifolds

The following algebraic description of hyperbolic manifolds and their frame bundle follows[DFG15, §3.1]. The universal model used here for hyperbolic manifolds isℍn:={x∈ℝn+1|⟨x,x⟩𝕄:=x02−∑j=1nxj2=1,x0>0}.\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{n}:=\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,|\,\langle x,x\rangle_{\mathbb{M}}:=x_{0}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{2}=1,x_{0}>0\bigr\}.

We will assume from now on thatn≥3n\geq 3. The group of orientation preserving isometries ofℍn\mathbb{H}^{n}isP​S​O​(1,n)=S​O​(1,n)/{±I​d}PSO(1,n)=SO(1,n)/\{\pm Id\}. Its Lie algebra can be described as follows(3.4)𝔰​𝔬​(1,n)\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}(1,n)={(01×100𝐤):𝐤∈𝔰​𝔬​(n)}⊕{(0𝐩⊤𝐩0):𝐩∈ℝn}\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\begin{pmatrix}0_{1\times 1}&0\\
0&\mathbf{k}\end{pmatrix}:\mathbf{k}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)\Bigr\}\oplus\Bigl\{\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{p}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{p}&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\Bigr\}:=𝔤′⊕𝔭,\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{p},

where𝔰​𝔬​(n)\mathfrak{so}(n)is the set of antisymmetric matrices ofℝn+1\mathbb{R}^{n+1}. This subgroup is the isotropy group of the elemente0=(1,0,…,0)∈ℍne_{0}=(1,0,\dotsc,0)\in\mathbb{H}^{n}. We thus have the identificationP​S​O​(1,n)/G′≅ℍnPSO(1,n)/G^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{H}^{n}, and seeingS​O​(n−1)SO(n-1)as a subgroupGGofG′G^{\prime}gives thatP​S​O​(1,n)/G≅S​ℍnPSO(1,n)/G\cong S\mathbb{H}^{n}. The following subalgebra of𝔰​𝔬​(1,n)\mathfrak{so}(1,n)is of importance𝔞:={(0𝐭⊤𝐭0n,n),𝐭=(t,…,0,0)⊤,t∈ℝ}.\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}:=\Bigl\{\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{t}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{t}&0_{n,n}\end{pmatrix},\mathbf{t}=(t,\dotsc,0,0)^{\top},t\in\mathbb{R}\Bigr\}.

## 3.1.2.The setting for the Borel-Weil calculus

As explained in Section2, the Borel-Weil calculus of[CL24]is conditionned by a choice of a horizontal distribution on the principal bundleP→MP\to M. In our case, the connection we consider for theS​O​(n−1)SO(n-1)principal bundleF​M→S​MFM\to SMis the dynamical connectionℍF​M→S​Mdyn=ℝ​XF​M⊕EF​Mu⊕EF​Ms\mathbb{H}^{\mathrm{dyn}}_{FM\to SM}=\mathbb{R}X_{FM}\oplus E^{u}_{FM}\oplus E^{s}_{FM}, defined in (3.3). Since this is the only principal connection we will consider in this note, we will omit most of the time the ’dyn’ superscript. We writeΘdyn\Theta_{\mathrm{dyn}}the corresponding connection 1-form onF​MFM, with values in𝔰​𝔬​(n−1)\mathfrak{so}(n-1).

Let us stress that there existsa priorianother canonical distinct choice of horizontal distribution onF​M→S​MFM\to SM, this choice will have a geometric nature whereas the dynamical connection is dynamical. Indeed, the Levi-Civita connection onT​M→MTM\to Minduces a vector connection on the normal bundle𝒩→S​M\mathcal{N}\to SMas explained in the beginning of Section3.1. We can parallel transport element ofF​MFM(a frame of𝒩\mathcal{N}) along any geodesic path onS​MSMfor the Sasaki metric. We writeτγL​C\tau^{LC}_{\gamma}such a parallel transport map. Then forp∈F​Mp\in FM, setℍF​M→S​ML​C​(p):={∂t|t=0⁡τγL​C​(p),γ:(−ε,ε)→M​geodesic s.t​(γ​(0),γ˙​(0))=(x,v)},\displaystyle\ \mathbb{H}_{FM\to SM}^{LC}(p):=\biggl\{{\partial_{t}}_{|t=0}\tau^{LC}_{\gamma}(p),\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to M\text{ geodesic s.t }(\gamma(0),\dot{\gamma}(0))=(x,v)\biggr\},

withπF​M→S​M​(p)=(x,v)\pi_{FM\to SM}(p)=(x,v). Thus the Levi-Civita connection on𝒩\mathcal{N}induces aS​O​(n−1)SO(n-1)principal bundle connection onF​M→S​MFM\to SM.

This connectiona prioridoes not coincide with the dynamical connection onF​MFM(3.3). Indeed, the Levi-Civita connection is always smooth whereas the dynamical connection (onF​MFM) is merely continuous. But the dynamical connection is smooth here, see (3.1.1) for the algebraic description ofEF​Mu,sE^{u,s}_{FM}. There is even more: a concrete computation allows to see that those two connections coincide. We will thus simply writeℍF​M→S​M\mathbb{H}_{FM\to SM}the principal bundle connection under consideration. The following remark justifies the previous affirmation.

## Remark 4.2.

LetN∈ℝ∗+N\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{*}. In the following pages, we may assume thatu=−Nu=-N,s=Ns=Nand in this case we will writeℋh,holN:=𝐀h,𝐤​(𝐦−N,N)−1​L2\mathcal{H}^{N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}:=\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{m}_{-N,N})^{-1}L^{2}. Thus for instanceℋh,holN\mathcal{H}^{N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}is microlocally equivalent toHh,holNH^{N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}(resp.Hh,hol−NH^{-N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}) nearEs,F∗E_{s,F}^{*}(resp. nearEu,F∗E_{u,F}^{*}). Here and in the rest of the paper we may only consider order functions of the type𝐦−N,N\mathbf{m}_{-N,N}(written𝐦N\mathbf{m}_{N}for simplicity), except in the following instance.

As in[DGH21, Lemma 4.9]or[CG21, Proposition 4.6]for instance, we will at some point need an unstable horocyclic invariance property of particular elements inℋh,holN\mathcal{H}^{N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}calledresonances. After applying such a horocyclic operator, we obtain an element ofℋh,hol[−N−1,N−1]=:ℋh,hol′⁣N\mathcal{H}^{[-N-1,N-1]}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}=:\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}, its order function is𝐦N′:=𝐦N−1/N\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{N}:=\mathbf{m}_{N}-1/Nwith escape function𝐆′:=𝐆𝐦N′\mathbf{G}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{N}}. The latter function is not an order function in the precise sense of Proposition4.1since it is not zero anymore nearE0∗E_{0}^{*}but one can check that the construction still works when requiring that𝐦\mathbf{m}is non-zero nearE0∗E_{0}^{*}with value strictly betweenuuandss, see[Cek22, Lemma 8.6]. In order to still have thatHξ​(XF)ω0​𝐆′H_{\xi(X_{F})}^{\omega_{0}}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}is strictly negative near the stable and unstable codirections, it is important thatN>1N>1(see the decay property of Proposition4.1). In this case,ℋh,hol′⁣N\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}still define an anisotropic space on which the following spectral properties of𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}remain valid.

The following is the main result of[FS11, §4.1.2]and[Lef26, §9.1], which stated in the setting of the Borel-Weil calculus corresponds to[CL24, §4.3.1].

## Proposition 4.3.

The operator(𝐗𝐤+z)(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}+z)is, restricted to its canonical domain onℋh,hol[u,s]\mathcal{H}^{[u,s]}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}, Fredholm of index0in the band{ℜ⁡(z)>−min⁡(−u,s)}\bigl\{\Re(z)>-\min(-u,s)\}. The resolvent(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}is well-defined and holomorphic forℜ⁡(z)≫0\Re(z)\gg 0as an operator onℋh,hol[u,s]​(F,𝐋𝐤)\mathcal{H}^{[u,s]}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}). Thus by the analytic Fredholm Theorem[CL24, Section 21.1.4.2],(−𝐗𝐤,Dℋ[u,s]​(𝐗𝐤))(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}},D_{\mathcal{H}^{[u,s]}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}))has a pure-point spectrum in the region{ℜ⁡(z)>−min⁡(u,s)}\bigl\{\Re(z)>-\min(u,s)\}, independent of the choice made in the construction.

We now work only withℋN\mathcal{H}^{N}in view of the previous remark (the case ofℋ′⁣N\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}is dealt with exactly in the same way). A pointz0∈ℂz_{0}\in\mathbb{C}will then be called aresonanceif there existsN>0N>0large enough such that it is a pole ofz↦(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1z\mapsto(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}onℋh,holu,s\mathcal{H}^{u,s}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}. Alternatively, it is a resonance if and only ifker𝒟Eu∗′⁡(𝐗𝐤+z0)≠0\ker_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{E_{u}^{*}}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}+z_{0})\neq 0. The elements of the previous set are calledresonant states. Finally, a distributionu∈ℋNu\in\mathcal{H}^{N}is called a generalized state atz0z_{0}ifu∈ker(𝐗𝐤+z0)Ju\in\ker(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}+z_{0})^{J}forJ≥2J\geq 2. The set of all resonances of𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}is writtenσPR​(𝐗𝐤)\sigma_{\mathrm{PR}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}).

## Remark 4.4.

It could be tempting to try to define anisotropic spaces onF​MFMdirectly. Indeed the Hamiltonian function(x,ξ)↦ξ​(XF​M​(x))(x,\xi)\mapsto\xi(X_{FM}(x))actually presents a sink-source structure on the whole ofT∗​(F​M)T^{*}(FM), replacingE0∗E_{0}^{*}byE0∗⊕𝕍F​M∗E_{0}^{*}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{FM}^{*}. However, the lack of ellipticity ofXF​MX_{FM}in the vertical (co)directions prevents from generalizing the proof from[Lef26, §9.1]. This is why the holomorphicity is key in[CL24, §4](and hence in our paper): it allows to get rid of the vertical codirections, at the cost of performing a Fourier decomposition.

Also, notice the following important fact. The identification (2.9) allows to realize an element of𝒟hol′​(F,𝐋𝐤)\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})as a distributional section in𝒟′​(S​M,H0​(F,𝐋𝐤))\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(SM,H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})). Through this identification,𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}is identified with∇XH0​(F,𝐋𝐤)\nabla^{H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})}_{X}. Let us justify this assertion.

## Remark 4.6.

The operator𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}acts on fiberwise holomorphic sections of𝐋𝐤→F\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\to Fand thus depend on a choice of maximal torusTT. However, the set of resonances does not depends on the chosen maximal torus. This can easily be seen by the fact that resonances of𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}correspond to resonances onH0​(F,𝐋𝐤)H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})as explained above, which further correspond to resonances onEλ​(𝐤)E^{\lambda(\mathbf{k})}by the Borel-Weil theorem, see the definition of the fiberwise Fourier transform (2.8). Indeed, the isomorphism of representation betweenH0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤)H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})andVλ​(𝐤)V^{\lambda(\mathbf{k})}induce an isomorphism of the associated vector bundles. This isomorphism will intertwin the natural associated connection onS​MSM.

Finally, the Borel-Weil calculus clearly generalizes in a vector valued setting as noticed in[CL24, Remark 3.3.17]. We can define the classΨh,BW​(F​M,E1→E2)\Psi_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(FM,E_{1}\to E_{2})forE1,E2E_{1},E_{2}two fiberwise holomorphic vector bundles overFF. Thus for instance, up to taking an invertible, self-adjoint and elliptic lower order perturbation𝐀h,𝐤​(mu,s,E1)\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(m_{u,s},E_{1})ofOph,BW⁡(IdE1​eGmu,s)\operatorname{Op}_{h,\mathrm{BW}}(\mathrm{Id}_{E_{1}}e^{G_{m_{u,s}}}), we can define the anisotropic space(4.6)ℋh,hol[u,s]​(F,𝐋𝐤⊗E1):=𝐀h,𝐤​(mu,s,E1)−1​L2​(F,𝐋𝐤⊗E1).\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{[u,s]}_{h,\mathrm{hol}}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes E_{1}):=\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(m_{u,s},E_{1})^{-1}L^{2}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes E_{1}).

A convention.We write𝐀h,𝐤N:=𝐀h,𝐤N​(𝐦−N,N)\mathbf{A}^{N}_{h,\mathbf{k}}:=\mathbf{A}^{N}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{m}_{-N,N})and𝐀h,𝐤′⁣N:=𝐀h,𝐤N​(𝐦−N−1,N−1)\mathbf{A}^{\prime N}_{h,\mathbf{k}}:=\mathbf{A}^{N}_{h,\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{m}_{-N-1,N-1})the anisotropic operators corresponding respectively toℋN\mathcal{H}^{N}andℋ′⁣N\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}, see Remark4.2. In the twisted case (by a fiberwise holomorphic vector bundleEEsay), we will write the previous operators𝐀h,𝐤N​(E)\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}}^{N}(E)and𝐀h,𝐤′⁣N​(E)\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}}^{\prime N}(E).

## 4.1.1.Anisotropic spaces forXF​MX_{FM}

After performing a Fourier decomposition of the operatorXF​MX_{FM}as a family of operators in the Borel-Weil calculus, we can go in the other direction to define functional spaces adapted toXF​MX_{FM}. They were introduced in[DGH21, Section 4.1]in the case of circle extensions and defined in full generality in[CL24, Section 4.4.1].

## Definition 4.7.

We define the following bi-graded norm, for anyf∈C∞​(F​M)f\in C^{\infty}(FM)‖f‖ℋm,s2:=∑𝐤∈G^d𝐤​⟨𝐤⟩2​m​∑i=1d𝐤‖f𝐤,i‖ℋ⟨𝐤⟩−1,hols2.\displaystyle||f||^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{m,s}}:=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\hat{G}}d_{\mathbf{k}}\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle^{2m}\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\mathbf{k}}}||f_{\mathbf{k},i}||^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle^{-1},\mathrm{hol}}}.

In turn,ℋs,m​(F​M)\mathcal{H}^{s,m}(FM)is defined as the completion ofC∞​(F​M)C^{\infty}(FM)for the previous norm.

The technical result leading to Theorem1.3We may now state the main technical theorem that will allow us to obtain the resolvent estimate in Theorem1.3. It is a component-wise estimate for the resolvent of𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}on the anisotropic spaces defined Proposition4.3. Recall first notationℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon)defined in (1.6), withε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1).

## Theorem 4.8.

For anyk∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}and anyε>0\varepsilon>0, the set⋃𝐤∈ΛσPR​(𝐗𝐤)\bigcup_{\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda}\sigma_{\mathrm{PR}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}})is finite inℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon). ## Remark 4.9.

Note that estimate (4.7) is stated forzzwith large enough imaginary part. Actually using the Quantum-Classical correspondence as in[DGH21, Appendix A], one can expect that the resonances in eachℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon)have zero imaginary part. Also, since⋃𝐤∈ΛσPR​(𝐗𝐤)\bigcup_{\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda}\sigma_{\mathrm{PR}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}})is finite in each strip, the estimate holds in the wholeℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon)for⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\ranglelarge enough.

Finally, we stated resolvent estimate withℜ⁡(z)≤0\Re(z)\leq 0, but inspecting the proof in Section5we can actually extend to anyℜ⁡(z)≥0\Re(z)\geq 0: the exponent over⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\ranglewill simply be11.

We may now justify how Theorem4.8implies Theorem1.3. The following sections will then be devoted to the proof of Theorem4.8

## 5.Resolvent estimates of𝐗𝐤\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}inℜ⁡(z)>−1\Re(z)>-1

## 5.1.The semiclassical formulation

Takeε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1)and write{βj}j≤k​(ε)\{\beta_{j}\}_{j\leq k(\varepsilon)}to be the finite set of all the poles ofz↦(−XF​M−z)−1z\mapsto(-X_{FM}-z)^{-1}in the bandℬ0​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{0}(\varepsilon), see Section1for the notation. That this set is finite is justified at the end of Section5.2. We defineλε:=maxj≤k​(ε)⁡|ℑ⁡(βj)|,\displaystyle\lambda_{\varepsilon}:=\max_{j\leq k(\varepsilon)}|\Im(\beta_{j})|,

allowing us to define the region where we will perform the resolvent estimates, namelyℬ0​(ε)′:=ℬ0​(ε)∩{|ℑ⁡(z)|≥λε+1}\mathcal{B}_{0}(\varepsilon)^{\prime}:=\mathcal{B}_{0}(\varepsilon)\,\cap\,\{|\Im(z)|\geq\lambda_{\varepsilon}+1\}. This restriction is only a matter of notational convenience, we do not seek to optimize the region where we prove resolvent estimates; also as explained in Remark4.9, for⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\ranglelarge enough the estimate can be proved on the whole stripℬ0​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{0}(\varepsilon).

## Remark 5.1.

Actually, using the paper[DFG15], we can strongly suspect thatλε\lambda_{\varepsilon}is0. For instance in then=3n=3case, the work[DGH21, Appendix A]proves thatλε=0\lambda_{\varepsilon}=0. However the proof should rely on thequantum-classical correspondence, which is far from understood outside of the setting of locally symmetric spaces. Since we want this paper to be as self-contained as possible, we have to accept the lack on knowledge onλε\lambda_{\varepsilon}.

In order to prove the estimate in Theorem4.8, we can prove two separate estimates (as done in[CL24, Section 4.5.1]to obtain resolvent bound on{ℜ⁡(z)≥0}\{\Re(z)\geq 0\})

## Proposition 5.2.

LetN>1N>1. There existsCεC_{\varepsilon}such that for allz∈ℬ0​(ε)′∩{ℜ⁡(z)∈(−1,0]}z\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\varepsilon)^{\prime}\cap\{\Re(z)\in(-1,0]\}(5.1)‖(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1‖ℋ⟨𝐤⟩−1,holN\displaystyle\|(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{N}_{\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle^{-1},\mathrm{hol}}}≤Cε​⟨𝐤⟩,\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle,if​⟨𝐤⟩≥⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩,\displaystyle\text{if }\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle\geq\langle\Im(z)\rangle,(5.2)‖(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1‖ℋ⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩−1,holN\displaystyle\|(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{N}_{\langle\Im(z)\rangle^{-1},\mathrm{hol}}}≤Cε​⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩,\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\langle\Im(z)\rangle,if​⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩≥⟨𝐤⟩.\displaystyle\text{if }\langle\Im(z)\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle.

whereNNis chosen strictly greater than−⌈ℜ⁡(z)⌉-\lceil\Re(z)\rceil.

Estimate (5.1) (resp. (5.2)) will be called thelarge⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\rangleregime(resp.the large⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩\langle\Im(z)\rangleregime). Let us quickly justify why (5.1) and (5.2) imply estimate (4.7). This is also justified in[CL24, Section 4.5.1], but for the reader’s convenience, we repeat the argument. That (5.1) implies (4.7) is clear because⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩≥1\langle\Im(z)\rangle\geq 1. In the case of the estimate (5.2), we use the following equivalence of norms (appearing for instance in[DGH21, Lemma 4.3]and[CL24, Proposition 4.5.1]), there existsC>0C>0such that for allh,h′>0h,h^{\prime}>0‖uh‖ℋhN≤C​(max⁡{h,h′}min⁡{h,h′})N​‖uh‖ℋh′N,\displaystyle\|u_{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{h}^{N}}\leq C\left(\frac{\max\{h,h^{\prime}\}}{\min\{h,h^{\prime}\}}\right)^{N}\|u_{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{h^{\prime}}^{N}},

which translates in the caseℑ⁡(z)≥⟨𝐤⟩\Im(z)\geq\langle\mathbf{k}\rangleas‖(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1‖ℋ⟨𝐤⟩−1,holN\displaystyle\|(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{N}_{\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle^{-1},\mathrm{hol}}}≤⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩N⟨𝐤⟩N×‖(−𝐗𝐤−z)−1‖ℋ⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩−1,holN\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{\langle\Im(z)\rangle^{N}}{\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle^{N}}\times\|(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}}-z)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{N}_{\langle\Im(z)\rangle^{-1},\mathrm{hol}}}≤Cε​⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩N+1\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\langle\Im(z)\rangle^{N+1}≤Cε​⟨𝐤⟩​⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩N+1\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle\langle\Im(z)\rangle^{N+1}

To prove the resolvent estimate inℬ0​(ε)′\mathcal{B}_{0}(\varepsilon)^{\prime}, an additional difficulty will be that in dimensions greater than33, we also need to prove estimates in regions where{ℜ⁡(z)≤−1}\{\Re(z)\leq-1\}. ## Remark 5.7.

In the case of quasimodes inℋ′⁣N\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}, one needs to replace𝐀h,𝐤​(h)N\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}^{N}by𝐀h,𝐤​(h)′⁣N\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}^{\prime N}and that the microlocal regularity nearEs,F∗E_{s,F}^{*}(resp.Eu,F∗E_{u,F}^{*}) isHN−1H^{N-1}(resp.H−N−1H^{-N-1}). To still have the threshold condition (concerningν\nu) for the radial estimates in Proposition2.22, it is thus needed thatN>2N>2for the estimates.

Some important unstable vector fields.As in[DGH21, Lemma 4.8](although our approach is slightly different) we aim at constructing particular (local) unstable vector fields onFFwhose Hamiltonian vector fields with respect toω𝐥\omega_{\mathbf{l}}exhibits goodtransversality propertiestoK−ηK_{-\eta}. Before doing so, we draw the attention of the reader to the following key fact. Thatη\etacould be zero is allowed in this high tensor power regime. In that case, the semiclassical measure concentrates on{ξ​(X​(x))=0}\{\xi(X(x))=0\}. In that case, will aim at constructing (horizontal) unstable vector fieldsU∈C∞​(F,EFu)U\in C^{\infty}(F,E^{u}_{F})whose Hamiltonian vector fields are, on their support, transversal to the submanifoldK0↪ℍF∗K_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}. Such vector fields must satisfy (see Lemma5.14)ω𝐥​(Hξ​(U​(x))ω𝐥,Hξ​(S​(x))ω𝐥)≠0\omega_{\mathbf{l}}(H^{\omega_{\mathbf{l}}}_{\xi(U(x))},H^{\omega_{\mathbf{l}}}_{\xi(S(x))})\neq 0forSSsome stable vector field onFF. By (2.20), this quantity reads asω𝐥​(Hξ​(U​(x))ω𝐥,Hξ​(S​(x))ω𝐥)=ξ​([U,S]​(x))+i​𝐥⋅𝐅∇¯​(U​(x),S​(x)).\displaystyle\omega_{\mathbf{l}}(H^{\omega_{\mathbf{l}}}_{\xi(U(x))},H^{\omega_{\mathbf{l}}}_{\xi(S(x))})=\xi([U,S](x))+i\mathbf{l}\cdot\mathbf{F}_{\overline{\nabla}}(U(x),S(x)).

Fromη=0\eta=0, we deduce that the first term must be zero onK0K_{0}(the zero section ofT∗​FT^{*}F)i.e.the Hamiltonian vector field ofUUwith respect toω0\omega_{0}is tangent toK0K_{0}. So, in order to have the transversality property, it is of importance thath​𝐤​(h)→𝐥≠0h\mathbf{k}(h)\to\mathbf{l}\neq 0.

We will see that in the case where⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩\langle\Im(z)\rangleis large (see (5.2)),𝐥\mathbf{l}can be zero but this will be compensated byη≠0\eta\neq 0. This is where the contact property of the geodesic flow comes in. This mechanism already appears in[DGH21]and is clearly still of importance for us.

In the definition of the parameter𝐥=(l1,…,ld)\mathbf{l}=(l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d})in (5.3), although𝐥≠0\mathbf{l}\neq 0by definition of the large⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\rangleregime, some of its components may vanish. To take that into account, we will assume without losing generalities that its first component verifiesl1≠0l_{1}\neq 0: that will explain why we will mostly care about the curvature ofL1L_{1}, namely𝐅∇¯1\mathbf{F}_{\overline{\nabla}_{1}}.
We recall that a basic vector fieldYYonFFcan be written as a horizontal liftY:=Y1ℍFY:={Y}_{1}^{\mathbb{H}_{F}}(withY1Y_{1}a vector field onS​MSM).

We work here with principally diagonal operators, the usual semiclassical results exposed in Section2apply. The Calderon-Vaillancourt theorem for small enoughh>0h>0andt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]implies that there existsC>0C>0such that‖e−t​𝐗𝐤​(h)u​et​𝐗𝐤​(h)u​(𝐀h,𝐤​(h)′⁣N​(Es,F∗))−1​e−t​𝐗𝐤​(h)u​(𝐀h,𝐤​(h)′⁣N​(Es,F∗))−1‖L2\displaystyle\|e^{-t\mathbf{X}^{u}_{\mathbf{k}(h)}}e^{t\mathbf{X}^{u}_{\mathbf{k}(h)}}(\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}^{\prime N}(E_{s,F}^{*}))^{-1}\,e^{-t\mathbf{X}^{u}_{\mathbf{k}(h)}}(\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}^{\prime N}(E_{s,F}^{*}))^{-1}\|_{L^{2}}≤e−t​(1+C​h),\displaystyle\leq e^{-t}\,(1+Ch),

The semigroup property gives the following bound for allt∈ℝ+t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}‖e−t​𝐗𝐤​(h),u​et​𝐗𝐤​(h),u​𝐀h,𝐤​(h)′⁣N,Es,F∗​e−t​𝐗𝐤​(h),u​(𝐀h,𝐤​(h)′⁣N,Es,F∗)−1‖L2\displaystyle\|e^{-t\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{k}(h),u}}e^{t\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{k}(h),u}}\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}^{\prime N,E_{s,F}^{*}}e^{-t\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{k}(h),u}}(\mathbf{A}_{h,\mathbf{k}(h)}^{\prime N,E_{s,F}^{*}})^{-1}\|_{L^{2}}≤e−t​(1+C​h)t+1.\displaystyle\leq e^{-t}\,(1+Ch)^{t+1}.

The main consequence of the horocyclic invariance is the support and propagation properties that it implies on the semiclassical measureμ\mu.

Finally, takinga∈Cc∞​(ℍF∗∩T∗​Wk)a\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{H}_{F}^{*}\cap T^{*}W_{k})leads to (5.16).

Deriving the contradictionWe now gather all the previous results to derive the final contradiction. The overall strategy uses crucially the transversality of the unstable Hamiltonian vector fields to the trapped set (see[Dya16],[CG21]or[DGH21]where this strategy is used).

## 5.2.2.The large⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩\langle\Im(z)\ranglecase

Without loss in generalities, we prove as before our estimates in the domainℑ⁡(z)≥0\Im(z)\geq 0. Recall that this regime correspond to the case⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩≥⟨𝐤⟩\langle\Im(z)\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle. The line of reasoning is usual and goes as follows. The result of Lemma5.17still applies for the semiclassical measure associated to this sequence of resonant state and the end of the proof for the large⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\rangleapplies.

The case⟨ℑ⁡(zj)⟩≥⟨𝐤j⟩\langle\Im(z_{j})\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{k}_{j}\rangle.Again, if⟨ℑ⁡(zj)⟩\langle\Im(z_{j})\rangledid not tend to infinity, then𝐤j=𝐤0′\mathbf{k}_{j}=\mathbf{k}_{0}^{\prime}after extraction and we would contradict the meromorphic property of the resolvent onℬ′0​(ε)\mathcal{B^{\prime}}_{0}(\varepsilon). Thus we are in the setting of (5.23). The above justification for horoyclic invariance applies and the arguments of Section5.2.2carry as well.

The representation is(A​d​(G),𝔫−)(Ad(G),\mathfrak{n}^{-})is isomorphic to the vector representation ofG=S​O​(n−1)G=SO(n-1)onℂn−1\mathbb{C}^{n-1}. Indeed, an elementg∈S​O​(n−1)g\in SO(n-1)is canonically seen inP​S​O​(1,n)PSO(1,n)using the diagonal embedding to a block-diagonal matrixdiag⁡(1,1,g)\operatorname{diag}(1,1,g). Then, by (3.5), we may canonically identify𝐯∈ℝn−1\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}to a matrixA​(𝐯)∈𝔫−A(\mathbf{v})\in\mathfrak{n}^{-}. For allg∈Gg\in G,A​d​(g)​(A​(𝐯))=g​A​(𝐯)​g−1=A​(g​𝐯)Ad(g)(A(\mathbf{v}))=gA(\mathbf{v})g^{-1}=A(g\mathbf{v}):AAintertwins the standard vector representation and the adjoint representation on𝔫−\mathfrak{n}^{-}. In turn, this implies that(A​d​(G)∨,(𝔫ℂ−)∗)(Ad(G)^{\vee},(\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{\mathbb{C}})^{*})is isomorphic to the (dual of the) standard vector representation ofGGonℂn−1\mathbb{C}^{n-1}, which is irreducible sincen≥4n\geq 4. We note that this is simply a manifestation of the general fact that the normal bundle𝒩\mathcal{N}is isomorphic toES​MuE^{u}_{SM}(this isomorphism of representation descends to an isomorphism of associated vector bundle).

The tensor representationH0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤)⊗ℂ𝔫ℂ−H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{\mathbb{C}}may be decomposed in a, bounded uniformly in𝐤\mathbf{k}, number of factors by[GW09, Corollary 9.2.4](6.12)H0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤)⊗(𝔫ℂ−)∗≅⨁j=1dH0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤+𝐞j)​ifnis odd,\displaystyle H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes(\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{\mathbb{C}})^{*}\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{d}H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{e}_{j}})\,\,\text{if $n$ is odd},(6.13)H0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤)⊗(𝔫ℂ−)∗≅H0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤)⊕⨁j=1dH0​(G/T,𝐉𝐤+𝐞j)​ifnis even.\displaystyle H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes(\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{\mathbb{C}})^{*}\cong H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{d}H^{0}(G/T,\mathbf{J}^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{e}_{j}})\,\,\text{if $n$ is even}.

For simpler notations, we recall that we assume thatnnis odd (again, the even case is dealt with the same procedure). Thanks to (2.10), the decomposition descends to the level of the corresponding associated vector bundles, that is(6.14)Φ𝐤:H0​(F,𝐋𝐤)⊗Es∗​(ℂ)​⟶≅​⨁j=1dH0​(F,𝐋𝐤+𝐞j).\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathbf{k}}:H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes E_{s}^{*}(\mathbb{C})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{d}H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}).

Let us write the previous isomorphism of vector bundleΦ𝐤\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(from the left-hand side to the right-hand side). The anisotropic norm onℋ2:=ℋh′⁣N​(S​M,⨁j=1dH0​(F,𝐋𝐤+𝐞j))\mathcal{H}_{2}:=\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}_{h}(SM,\bigoplus_{j=1}^{d}H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}))is equivalent to the natural product norm induced by the ones from each anisotropic block. We write this product norm∥⋅∥2\|\cdot\|_{2}. This yields that there existsC>0C>0independent ofhhsuch that(6.16)||Φ∗𝐤∥ℋ1→ℋ2≤C.\displaystyle||\Phi^{\mathbf{k}}_{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}}\leq C.

For allj∈⟦1,d⟧j\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket, the induction hypothesis applies toPhH0​(F,𝐋𝐤+𝐞j)​(zh+i​h)P_{h}^{H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{e}_{j}})}(z_{h}+ih)sinceℜ⁡(zh)+1∈[−m+ε,−m+1]\Re(z_{h})+1\in[-m+\varepsilon,-m+1]. More precisely‖PhH0​(F,𝐋𝐤+𝐞j)​(zh+i​h)−1‖ℋh′⁣N​(H0​(F,𝐋𝐤)⊗Es∗)≲h−1​h⌊ℜ⁡(zh)⌋+1=h−m−1.\displaystyle\|P_{h}^{H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{e}_{j}})}(z_{h}+ih)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime N}_{h}(H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes E_{s}^{*})}\lesssim h^{-1}h^{\lfloor\Re(z_{h})\rfloor+1}=h^{-m-1}.

The continuity property (6.16) together with (6.15) gives the desired estimate.

## Proof.

The first items are proved in Proposition5.5. Concerning the horocyclic invariance property, we notice that the proof of Lemma6.11did not use the assumptionν>−n−12\nu>-\tfrac{n-1}{2}. In fact, the proof of the horocyclic invariance property of Section6.1carries directly for our sequence(uh)(u_{h}).
∎

In the caseν>−n−12\nu>-\tfrac{n-1}{2}, we used the Lipschitz upper bound forμ\mufrom Lemma5.17to deduce the contradiction in (5.22). Here, sinceν<−n−12\nu<-\tfrac{n-1}{2}, we will use instead the Lipschitz lower bound onμ\mufrom Lemma5.17.
We use the notation from the end of Section5. In particular, there exists a covering(Wi)(W_{i})ofFFsatisfying Lemma5.14, giving in turn the mappingψi\psi_{i}defined in (5.18). There exist constantsC1,C2>0C_{1},C_{2}>0such that(6.21)⋃iψi​(BC2​δ​e−ti)⊂UC1​δ0​e−t⊂Φ−tω0,p​(Uδ0),∀t>0,\displaystyle\bigcup_{i}\psi_{i}(B^{i}_{C_{2}\delta e^{-t}})\subset U_{C_{1}\delta_{0}e^{-t}}\subset\Phi_{-t}^{\omega_{0},p}(U_{\delta_{0}}),\forall t>0,

which gives for anyiithatμ​(Φ−tω0,p​(Uδ0))≥μ​(ψi​(BC2​δ​e−ti))\mu(\Phi_{-t}^{\omega_{0},p}(U_{\delta_{0}}))\geq\mu(\psi_{i}(B^{i}_{C_{2}\delta e^{-t}})). From (6.21), one uses the Lipschitz lower bound from Proposition5.17together with the propagation property of Proposition6.4to gete2​ν​t≳e−(n−1)​t,\displaystyle e^{2\nu t}\gtrsim e^{-(n-1)t},

with a time independant implicit constant. But since2​ν<−(n−1)2\nu<-(n-1), this is impossible. The large imaginary part regime follows exactly for the same reasons.


There is only a finite number of resonances inℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon)This is done by induction: assume that the set of resonances is finite inℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon). Once again the elements ofσPR​(𝐗𝐤)\sigma_{\mathrm{PR}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}})are written(zj,𝐤)(z_{j,\mathbf{k}})and the corresponding resonant states are(uj,𝐤)(u_{j,\mathbf{k}}).
Assume for the sake of contradiction that∪𝐤σPR​(𝐗𝐤)∩ℬk+1​(ε)\cup_{\mathbf{k}}\sigma_{\mathrm{PR}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}})\cap\mathcal{B}_{k+1}(\varepsilon)is infinite and write the corresponding sequence of resonant states(uh)h(u_{h})_{h}. We only deal with the large⟨𝐤⟩\langle\mathbf{k}\ranglecase as the large⟨ℑ⁡(z)⟩\langle\Im(z)\ranglecase follows similarly.

That⟨𝐤j⟩\langle\mathbf{k}_{j}\ranglemust go to infinity is justified at the end of Section5. Once again, we obtain a sequence of resonant states(uh)(u_{h})satisfying(−𝐗𝐤​(h)−zh)​uh=0(-\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{k}(h)}-z_{h})u_{h}=0. The only point that will differ with the case of the resolvent estimates concerns horocyclic invariance. The relation (6.10) yields thath​𝒰−​uhh\mathcal{U}^{-}u_{h}satisfies𝐏hH0​(F,𝐋𝐤)⊗Es∗​(zh+i​h)​(h​𝒰−​uh)=0\mathbf{P}^{H^{0}(F,\mathbf{L}^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes E_{s}^{*}}_{h}(z_{h}+ih)(h\mathcal{U}^{-}u_{h})=0. To apply the induction hypothesis, apply decomposition (6.12) or (6.13) depending on the parity ofnnto retrieve that the components ofh​𝒰−​uhh\mathcal{U}^{-}u_{h}are all eventually zero forhhsmall enough. Indeed, the components ofh​𝒰−​uhh\mathcal{U}^{-}u_{h}with respect to decomposition (6.12) are resonant states for𝐏h\mathbf{P}_{h}inℬk​(ε)\mathcal{B}_{k}(\varepsilon).
